					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content"><div class="mw-subjectpageheader">
</div><div class="mw-content-ltr mw-parser-output" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Scientific study of celestial objects</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1320445320">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}@media print{body.ns-0 .mw-parser-output .hatnote{display:none!important}}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable"><span>This article is about the scientific study of celestial objects; not to be confused with <a href="/wiki/Astrology" title="Astrology">Astrology</a>, a divinatory pseudoscience.</span> <span>For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Astronomy_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Astronomy (disambiguation)">Astronomy (disambiguation)</a>.</span></div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">


</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Laser_Towards_Milky_Ways_Centre.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Laser_Towards_Milky_Ways_Centre.jpg/500px-Laser_Towards_Milky_Ways_Centre.jpg" decoding="async" width="380" height="345" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Laser_Towards_Milky_Ways_Centre.jpg/960px-Laser_Towards_Milky_Ways_Centre.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="4000" data-file-height="3631" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Paranal_Observatory" title="Paranal Observatory">Paranal Observatory</a> of <a href="/wiki/European_Southern_Observatory" title="European Southern Observatory">European Southern Observatory</a> creating a <a href="/wiki/Laser_guide_star" title="Laser guide star">laser guide star</a> in the atmosphere to observe the <a href="/wiki/Galactic_Center" title="Galactic Center">Galactic Center</a></figcaption></figure>
<p><b>Astronomy</b> is a <a href="/wiki/Natural_science" title="Natural science">natural science</a> that studies <a href="/wiki/Astronomical_object" title="Astronomical object">celestial objects</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Phenomena" class="mw-redirect" title="Phenomena">phenomena</a> that occur in the <a href="/wiki/Cosmos" title="Cosmos">cosmos</a>. It uses <a href="/wiki/Mathematics" title="Mathematics">mathematics</a>, <a href="/wiki/Physics" title="Physics">physics</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Chemistry" title="Chemistry">chemistry</a> to explain their origin and their overall <a href="/wiki/Chronology_of_the_Universe" class="mw-redirect" title="Chronology of the Universe">evolution</a>. Objects of interest include <a href="/wiki/Planets" class="mw-redirect" title="Planets">planets</a>, <a href="/wiki/Natural_satellite" title="Natural satellite">moons</a>, <a href="/wiki/Star" title="Star">stars</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nebulae" class="mw-redirect" title="Nebulae">nebulae</a>, <a href="/wiki/Galaxy" title="Galaxy">galaxies</a>, <a href="/wiki/Meteoroid" title="Meteoroid">meteoroids</a>, <a href="/wiki/Asteroid" title="Asteroid">asteroids</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Comet" title="Comet">comets</a>. Relevant phenomena include <a href="/wiki/Supernova" title="Supernova">supernova</a> explosions, <a href="/wiki/Gamma_ray_burst" class="mw-redirect" title="Gamma ray burst">gamma ray bursts</a>, <a href="/wiki/Quasar" title="Quasar">quasars</a>, <a href="/wiki/Blazar" title="Blazar">blazars</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pulsar" title="Pulsar">pulsars</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Cosmic_microwave_background_radiation" class="mw-redirect" title="Cosmic microwave background radiation">cosmic microwave background radiation</a>. More generally, astronomy studies everything that originates beyond <a href="/wiki/Atmosphere_of_Earth" title="Atmosphere of Earth">Earth's atmosphere</a>. <a href="/wiki/Physical_cosmology" title="Physical cosmology">Cosmology</a> is the branch of astronomy that studies the <a href="/wiki/Universe" title="Universe">universe</a> as a whole.
</p><p>Astronomy is one of the oldest natural sciences. The early civilizations in <a href="/wiki/Recorded_history" title="Recorded history">recorded history</a> made methodical observations of the <a href="/wiki/Night_sky" title="Night sky">night sky</a>. These include the <a href="/wiki/Egyptian_astronomy" title="Egyptian astronomy">Egyptians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Babylonian_astronomy" title="Babylonian astronomy">Babylonians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Greek_astronomy" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek astronomy">Greeks</a>, <a href="/wiki/Indian_astronomy" title="Indian astronomy">Indians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chinese_astronomy" title="Chinese astronomy">Chinese</a>, <a href="/wiki/Maya_civilization" title="Maya civilization">Maya</a>, and many ancient <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas" title="Indigenous peoples of the Americas">indigenous peoples of the Americas</a>. In the past, astronomy included disciplines as diverse as <a href="/wiki/Astrometry" title="Astrometry">astrometry</a>, <a href="/wiki/Celestial_navigation" title="Celestial navigation">celestial navigation</a>, <a href="/wiki/Observational_astronomy" title="Observational astronomy">observational astronomy</a>, and the making of <a href="/wiki/Calendar" title="Calendar">calendars</a>.
</p><p>Astronomy is one of the few sciences in which amateurs play an <a href="/wiki/Citizen_science" title="Citizen science">active role</a>. This is especially true for the discovery and observation of <a href="/wiki/Transient_astronomical_event" class="mw-redirect" title="Transient astronomical event">transient events</a>. <a href="/wiki/Amateur_astronomy" title="Amateur astronomy">Amateur astronomers</a> have helped with many important discoveries, such as finding new comets.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Etymology">Etymology</h2></div>
<p><i>Astronomy</i> (from the <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greek" title="Ancient Greek">Greek</a> <a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E1%BC%80%CF%83%CF%84%CF%81%CE%BF%CE%BD%CE%BF%CE%BC%CE%AF%CE%B1#Ancient_Greek" class="extiw" title="wikt:ἀστρονομία">ἀστρονομία</a> from <a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E1%BC%84%CF%83%CF%84%CF%81%CE%BF%CE%BD#Ancient_Greek" class="extiw" title="wikt:ἄστρον">ἄστρον</a> <i>astron</i>, "star" and -νομία <i><a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/-nomy" class="extiw" title="wikt:-nomy">-nomia</a></i> from <a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%CE%BD%CF%8C%CE%BC%CE%BF%CF%82#Ancient_Greek" class="extiw" title="wikt:νόμος">νόμος</a> <i>nomos</i>, "law" or "rule") means study of celestial objects.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>1<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Astronomy should not be confused with <a href="/wiki/Astrology" title="Astrology">astrology</a>, the belief system which claims that human affairs are correlated with the positions of celestial objects. The <a href="/wiki/Astrology_and_astronomy" title="Astrology and astronomy">two fields</a> share a common origin but became distinct, astronomy being supported by <a href="/wiki/Physics" title="Physics">physics</a> while astrology is not.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>2<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Use_of_terms_&quot;astronomy&quot;_and_&quot;astrophysics&quot;"><span id="Use_of_terms_.22astronomy.22_and_.22astrophysics.22"></span>Use of terms "astronomy" and "astrophysics"</h3></div>
<p>"Astronomy" and "<a href="/wiki/Astrophysics" title="Astrophysics">astrophysics</a>" are broadly synonymous in modern usage.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-scharrinhausen&#95;3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-scharrinhausen-3"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>3<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-odenwald&#95;4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-odenwald-4"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>4<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-pennstateerie&#95;5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pennstateerie-5"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>5<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In dictionary definitions, "astronomy" is "the study of objects and matter outside the Earth's atmosphere and of their physical and chemical properties",<sup id="cite&#95;ref-mw-astronomy&#95;6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mw-astronomy-6"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>6<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> while "astrophysics" is the branch of astronomy dealing with "the behavior, physical properties, and dynamic processes of celestial objects and phenomena".<sup id="cite&#95;ref-mw-astrophysics&#95;7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mw-astrophysics-7"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>7<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Sometimes, as in the introduction of the introductory textbook <i>The Physical Universe</i> by <a href="/wiki/Frank_Shu" title="Frank Shu">Frank Shu</a>, "astronomy" means the qualitative study of the subject, whereas "astrophysics" is the physics-oriented version of the subject.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-shu1982&#95;8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-shu1982-8"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>8<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Some fields, such as <a href="/wiki/Astrometry" title="Astrometry">astrometry</a>, are in this sense purely astronomy rather than also astrophysics. Research departments may use "astronomy" and "astrophysics" according to whether the department is historically affiliated with a physics department,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-odenwald&#95;4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-odenwald-4"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>4<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and many professional <a href="/wiki/Astronomer" title="Astronomer">astronomers</a> have physics rather than astronomy degrees.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-pennstateerie&#95;5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pennstateerie-5"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>5<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Thus, in modern use, the two terms are often used interchangeably.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-scharrinhausen&#95;3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-scharrinhausen-3"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>3<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="History">History</h2></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_astronomy" title="History of astronomy">History of astronomy</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For a chronological guide, see <a href="/wiki/Timeline_of_astronomy" title="Timeline of astronomy">Timeline of astronomy</a>.</div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Pre-historic">Pre-historic</h3></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Megaliths_Aswan_Nubia_museum.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Megaliths_Aswan_Nubia_museum.JPG/250px-Megaliths_Aswan_Nubia_museum.JPG" decoding="async" width="250" height="166" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Megaliths_Aswan_Nubia_museum.JPG/500px-Megaliths_Aswan_Nubia_museum.JPG 1.5x" data-file-width="4288" data-file-height="2848" /></a><figcaption>Megaliths from <a href="/wiki/Nabta_Playa" title="Nabta Playa">Nabta Playa</a>, constructed by Neolithic populations,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>9<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> located in <a href="/wiki/Aswan" title="Aswan">Aswan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Upper_Egypt" title="Upper Egypt">Upper Egypt</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-ReferenceC&#95;10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceC-10"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>10<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1600_Himmelsscheibe_von_Nebra_sky_disk_anagoria.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/1600_Himmelsscheibe_von_Nebra_sky_disk_anagoria.jpg/250px-1600_Himmelsscheibe_von_Nebra_sky_disk_anagoria.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="250" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/1600_Himmelsscheibe_von_Nebra_sky_disk_anagoria.jpg/500px-1600_Himmelsscheibe_von_Nebra_sky_disk_anagoria.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="2724" data-file-height="2724" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Nebra_sky_disc" title="Nebra sky disc">Nebra sky disc</a> found on <a href="/wiki/Mittelberg_(Rh%C3%B6n)" title="Mittelberg (Rhön)">Mittenberg hill</a> in Germany and dated to <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1800–1600 BCE</span>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>11<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> </figcaption></figure>
<p>The initial development of astronomy was driven by practical needs like agricultural calendars. Before recorded history archeological sites such as <a href="/wiki/Stonehenge" title="Stonehenge">Stonehenge</a> provide evidence of ancient interest in astronomical observations.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Ryden-2020&#95;12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ryden-2020-12"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>12<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 15">&#58;&#8202;15&#8202;</span></sup> 
Evidence also comes from artefacts such as the <a href="/wiki/Nebra_sky_disc" title="Nebra sky disc">Nebra sky disc</a> inlaid with symbols interpreted as a sun, moon, and stars including <a href="/wiki/Pleiades" title="Pleiades">a cluster of seven stars</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Meller&#95;2021&#95;13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Meller_2021-13"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>13<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Halle&#95;14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Halle-14"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>14<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>15<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Megalithic structures located in <a href="/wiki/Nabta_Playa" title="Nabta Playa">Nabta Playa</a>, Upper Egypt featured astronomical calendar arrangements in alignment with the heliacal rising of <a href="/wiki/Sirius" title="Sirius">Sirius</a> and supported calibration the yearly calendar for the annual Nile flood.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>16<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Classical">Classical</h3></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ct-33-planisphere.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Ct-33-planisphere.jpg/250px-Ct-33-planisphere.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="248" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Ct-33-planisphere.jpg/500px-Ct-33-planisphere.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="806" data-file-height="800" /></a><figcaption>A Babylonian <a href="/wiki/Planisphere" title="Planisphere">planisphere</a> (7th century BCE) was an early astronomical instrument. Its use of <a href="/wiki/Sexagesimal" title="Sexagesimal">sexagesimals</a> (e.g. 12, 24, 60, 360) is still being used today through having been broadly adopted for <a href="/wiki/Timekeeping" class="mw-redirect" title="Timekeeping">timekeeping</a> and <a href="/wiki/Astrometry" title="Astrometry">astrometry</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-x754&#95;17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-x754-17"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>17<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Civilizations such as <a href="/wiki/Egyptian_astronomy" title="Egyptian astronomy">Egypt</a>, <a href="/wiki/Babylonian_astronomy" title="Babylonian astronomy">Mesopotamia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Greek_astronomy" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek astronomy">Greece</a>, <a href="/wiki/Indian_astronomy" title="Indian astronomy">India</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chinese_astronomy" title="Chinese astronomy">China</a> independently but with cross-cultural influences created astronomical observatories and developed ideas on the nature of the Universe, along with calendars and astronomical instruments.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Sarma&#95;2000&#95;18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sarma_2000-18"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>18<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> A key early development was the beginning of mathematical and scientific astronomy among the Babylonians, laying the foundations for astronomical traditions in other civilizations.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>19<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The Babylonians discovered that <a href="/wiki/Lunar_eclipses" class="mw-redirect" title="Lunar eclipses">lunar eclipses</a> recurred in the <a href="/wiki/Saros_cycle" class="mw-redirect" title="Saros cycle">saros cycle</a> of 223 <a href="/wiki/Synodic_month" class="mw-redirect" title="Synodic month">synodic months</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>20<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Following the Babylonians, significant advances were made in <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greece" title="Ancient Greece">ancient Greece</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Hellenistic_civilization" class="mw-redirect" title="Hellenistic civilization">Hellenistic world</a>. Greek astronomy sought a rational, physical explanation for celestial phenomena.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>21<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>  In the 4th century BC, <a href="/wiki/Heracleides_Ponticus" class="mw-redirect" title="Heracleides Ponticus">Heracleides Ponticus</a> was the first to proposed that the Earth rotates on its own axis.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>22<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In the 3rd century BC, <a href="/wiki/Aristarchus_of_Samos" title="Aristarchus of Samos">Aristarchus of Samos</a> estimated the <a href="/wiki/Aristarchus_On_the_Sizes_and_Distances" class="mw-redirect" title="Aristarchus On the Sizes and Distances">size and distance of the Moon and Sun</a>, and he proposed a model of the <a href="/wiki/Solar_System" title="Solar System">Solar System</a> where the Earth and planets rotated around the Sun, now called the <a href="/wiki/Heliocentrism" title="Heliocentrism">heliocentric</a> model.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>23<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In the 2nd century BC, <a href="/wiki/Hipparchus" title="Hipparchus">Hipparchus</a> calculated the size and distance of the Moon and invented the earliest known astronomical devices such as the <a href="/wiki/Astrolabe" title="Astrolabe">astrolabe</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>24<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> He also observed the small drift in the positions of the equinoxes and solstices with respect to the fixed stars that we now know is caused by <a href="/wiki/Precession" title="Precession">precession</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Ryden-2020&#95;12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ryden-2020-12"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>12<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Hipparchus also created a catalog of 1020 stars, and most of the <a href="/wiki/Constellation" title="Constellation">constellations</a> of the northern hemisphere derive from Greek astronomy.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>25<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Antikythera_mechanism" title="Antikythera mechanism">Antikythera mechanism</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;150</span>–80 BC) was an early <a href="/wiki/Analog_computer" title="Analog computer">analog computer</a> designed to calculate the location of the <a href="/wiki/Sun" title="Sun">Sun</a>, <a href="/wiki/Moon" title="Moon">Moon</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Planets" class="mw-redirect" title="Planets">planets</a> for a given date. Technological artifacts of similar complexity did not reappear until the 14th century, when mechanical <a href="/wiki/Astronomical_clock" title="Astronomical clock">astronomical clocks</a> appeared in Europe.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-insearchoflosttime&#95;26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-insearchoflosttime-26"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>26<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Post-classical">Post-classical</h3></div>
<p>After the classical Greek era, astronomy was dominated by the <a href="/wiki/Geocentric_model" class="mw-redirect" title="Geocentric model">geocentric model</a> of the Universe, or the <a href="/wiki/Ptolemaic_system" class="mw-redirect" title="Ptolemaic system">Ptolemaic system</a>, named after <a href="/wiki/Claudius_Ptolemy" class="mw-redirect" title="Claudius Ptolemy">Claudius Ptolemy</a>. His 13-volume astronomy work, named the <i><a href="/wiki/Almagest" title="Almagest">Almagest</a></i> in its Arabic translation, became the primary reference for over a thousand years.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Christian-2010&#95;27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Christian-2010-27"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>27<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 196">&#58;&#8202;196&#8202;</span></sup>  In this system, the Earth was believed to be the center of the Universe with the Sun, the Moon and the stars rotating around it.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>28<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> While the system would eventually be discredited, it gave the most accurate predictions for the positions of astronomical bodies available at that time.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Christian-2010&#95;27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Christian-2010-27"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>27<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>With the arrival of Hellenistic astronomy in India through trade and cultural contacts, Indian astronomy entered a new phase during the early centuries CE.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>29<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>  Earlier indigenous traditions, such as those recorded in the Vedāṅga Jyotiṣa, provided calendrical foundations, while Greek astronomical models were later integrated by scholars including <a href="/wiki/%C4%80ryabha%E1%B9%ADa" class="mw-redirect" title="Āryabhaṭa">Āryabhaṭa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Var%C4%81hamihira" title="Varāhamihira">Varāhamihira</a>, and Brahmagupta.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Pingree&#95;(1978),&#95;533,&#95;554f.&#95;30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pingree_(1978),_533,_554f.-30"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>30<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Āryabhaṭa notably improved methods for calculating planetary motions and eclipses.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>31<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In the later medieval period, the <a href="/wiki/Kerala_school_of_astronomy_and_mathematics" title="Kerala school of astronomy and mathematics">Kerala school</a> contributed to astronomy through refined observational practices and more accurate planetary and eclipse calculations.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>32<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Al-_Farg%C4%81n%C4%AB,_A%E1%B8%A5mad_ibn_Mu%E1%B8%A5ammad_%E2%80%93_Compilatio_astronomica,_1493_%E2%80%93_BEIC_13262685.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Al-_Farg%C4%81n%C4%AB%2C_A%E1%B8%A5mad_ibn_Mu%E1%B8%A5ammad_%E2%80%93_Compilatio_astronomica%2C_1493_%E2%80%93_BEIC_13262685.jpg/250px-Al-_Farg%C4%81n%C4%AB%2C_A%E1%B8%A5mad_ibn_Mu%E1%B8%A5ammad_%E2%80%93_Compilatio_astronomica%2C_1493_%E2%80%93_BEIC_13262685.jpg" decoding="async" width="190" height="245" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Al-_Farg%C4%81n%C4%AB%2C_A%E1%B8%A5mad_ibn_Mu%E1%B8%A5ammad_%E2%80%93_Compilatio_astronomica%2C_1493_%E2%80%93_BEIC_13262685.jpg/330px-Al-_Farg%C4%81n%C4%AB%2C_A%E1%B8%A5mad_ibn_Mu%E1%B8%A5ammad_%E2%80%93_Compilatio_astronomica%2C_1493_%E2%80%93_BEIC_13262685.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Al-_Farg%C4%81n%C4%AB%2C_A%E1%B8%A5mad_ibn_Mu%E1%B8%A5ammad_%E2%80%93_Compilatio_astronomica%2C_1493_%E2%80%93_BEIC_13262685.jpg/500px-Al-_Farg%C4%81n%C4%AB%2C_A%E1%B8%A5mad_ibn_Mu%E1%B8%A5ammad_%E2%80%93_Compilatio_astronomica%2C_1493_%E2%80%93_BEIC_13262685.jpg 2x" data-file-width="899" data-file-height="1161" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of <a href="/wiki/Alfraganus" class="mw-redirect" title="Alfraganus">Alfraganus</a> in the <i>Compilatio astronomica</i>, 1493. <a href="/wiki/Islamic_astronomy" class="mw-redirect" title="Islamic astronomy">Islamic astronomers</a> collected and translated <a href="/wiki/Indian_astronomy" title="Indian astronomy">Indian</a>, <a href="/wiki/Persian_astronomy" title="Persian astronomy">Persian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Greek_astronomy" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek astronomy">Greek</a> texts, adding their own work.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-n063&#95;33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-n063-33"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>33<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Astronomy_in_the_medieval_Islamic_world" title="Astronomy in the medieval Islamic world">Astronomy flourished in the medieval Islamic world</a>. Astronomical <a href="/wiki/Observatory" title="Observatory">observatories</a> were established there by the early 9th century.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Kennedy-1962&#95;34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kennedy-1962-34"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>34<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Micheau-992-3&#95;35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Micheau-992-3-35"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>35<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>36<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In 964, the <a href="/wiki/Andromeda_Galaxy" title="Andromeda Galaxy">Andromeda Galaxy</a>, the largest <a href="/wiki/Galaxy" title="Galaxy">galaxy</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Local_Group" title="Local Group">Local Group</a>, was described by the Persian Muslim astronomer <a href="/wiki/Abd_al-Rahman_al-Sufi" title="Abd al-Rahman al-Sufi">Abd al-Rahman al-Sufi</a> in his <i><a href="/wiki/Book_of_Fixed_Stars" class="mw-redirect" title="Book of Fixed Stars">Book of Fixed Stars</a></i>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-NSOG&#95;37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NSOG-37"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>37<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/SN_1006" title="SN 1006">SN 1006</a> <a href="/wiki/Supernova" title="Supernova">supernova</a>, the brightest <a href="/wiki/Apparent_magnitude" title="Apparent magnitude">apparent magnitude</a> stellar event in the last 1000 years, was observed by the Egyptian Arabic astronomer <a href="/wiki/Ali_ibn_Ridwan" title="Ali ibn Ridwan">Ali ibn Ridwan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Chinese_astronomy" title="Chinese astronomy">Chinese astronomers</a> in 1006.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>38<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Iranian scholar <a href="/wiki/Al-Biruni" title="Al-Biruni">Al-Biruni</a> observed that, contrary to <a href="/wiki/Ptolemy" title="Ptolemy">Ptolemy</a>, the Sun's <a href="/wiki/Apogee" class="mw-redirect" title="Apogee">apogee</a> (highest point in the heavens) was mobile, not fixed.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Goldstein1967&#95;39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Goldstein1967-39"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>39<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>40<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Arabic astronomers introduced many <a href="/wiki/List_of_Arabic_star_names" title="List of Arabic star names">Arabic names now used for individual stars</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>41<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The ruins at <a href="/wiki/Great_Zimbabwe" title="Great Zimbabwe">Great Zimbabwe</a> and <a href="/wiki/Timbuktu" title="Timbuktu">Timbuktu</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>42<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> may have housed astronomical observatories.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>43<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In Post-classical West Africa, astronomers studied the movement of stars and relation to seasons, crafting charts of the heavens and diagrams of orbits of the other planets based on complex mathematical calculations.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>44<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Songhai_Empire" title="Songhai Empire">Songhai</a> historian <a href="/wiki/Mahmud_Kati" title="Mahmud Kati">Mahmud Kati</a> documented a <a href="/wiki/Meteor_shower" title="Meteor shower">meteor shower</a> in 1583.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>45<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>In medieval Europe, <a href="/wiki/Richard_of_Wallingford" title="Richard of Wallingford">Richard of Wallingford</a> (1292–1336) invented the first astronomical clock, the <a href="/wiki/Rectangulus" title="Rectangulus">Rectangulus</a> which allowed for the measurement of angles between planets and other astronomical bodies,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Gimpel&#95;1992&#95;46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gimpel_1992-46"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>46<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> as well as an <a href="/wiki/Equatorium" title="Equatorium">equatorium</a> called the <i>Albion</i> which could be used for astronomical calculations such as <a href="/wiki/Moon" title="Moon">lunar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sun" title="Sun">solar</a> and <a href="/wiki/Planet" title="Planet">planetary</a> <a href="/wiki/Longitude" title="Longitude">longitudes</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>47<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Nicole_Oresme" title="Nicole Oresme">Nicole Oresme</a> (1320–1382) discussed evidence for the rotation of the Earth.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>48<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Jean_Buridan" title="Jean Buridan">Jean Buridan</a> (1300–1361) developed the <a href="/wiki/Theory_of_impetus" title="Theory of impetus">theory of impetus</a>, describing motions including of the celestial bodies.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>49<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>50<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
For over six centuries (from the recovery of ancient learning during the late Middle Ages into the Enlightenment), the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic Church</a> gave more financial and social support to the study of astronomy than probably all other institutions. Among the Church's motives was finding the <a href="/wiki/Date_of_Easter" title="Date of Easter">date for Easter</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>51<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Copernicus">Copernicus</h3></div>
<p>During the <a href="/wiki/Renaissance" title="Renaissance">Renaissance</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nicolaus_Copernicus" title="Nicolaus Copernicus">Nicolaus Copernicus</a> proposed a heliocentric model of the solar system.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>52<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> While his model maintained circular orbits, it was sufficient to calculate the size of planetary orbits and their period. The appealing simplicity of Copernican astronomy led to its adoption among astronomers even before it was confirmed by Galileo's telescopic observations in the 1600s.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Ryden-2020&#95;12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ryden-2020-12"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>12<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 40">&#58;&#8202;40&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Early_telescopic">Early telescopic</h3></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Galileo%27s_sketches_of_the_moon.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/Galileo%27s_sketches_of_the_moon.png/250px-Galileo%27s_sketches_of_the_moon.png" decoding="async" width="190" height="263" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/Galileo%27s_sketches_of_the_moon.png/330px-Galileo%27s_sketches_of_the_moon.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/Galileo%27s_sketches_of_the_moon.png/500px-Galileo%27s_sketches_of_the_moon.png 2x" data-file-width="2200" data-file-height="3050" /></a><figcaption>The first sketches of the Moon's topography, from <a href="/wiki/Galileo" class="mw-redirect" title="Galileo">Galileo</a>'s ground-breaking <i><a href="/wiki/Sidereus_Nuncius" title="Sidereus Nuncius">Sidereus Nuncius</a></i> (1610)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Sometime around 1608 the telescope was invented and by 1610, <a href="/wiki/Galileo_Galilei" title="Galileo Galilei">Galileo Galilei</a> observed phases on the planet <a href="/wiki/Venus" title="Venus">Venus</a> similar to those of the Moon, supporting the heliocentric model.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Ryden-2020&#95;12-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ryden-2020-12"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>12<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Around the same time the heliocentric model was organized quantitatively by <a href="/wiki/Johannes_Kepler" title="Johannes Kepler">Johannes Kepler</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>53<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Analyzing two decades of careful observations by <a href="/wiki/Tycho_Brahe" title="Tycho Brahe">Tycho Brahe</a>, Kepler devised a system that described the details of the motion of the planets around the Sun.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Longair-2023&#95;54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longair-2023-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 4">&#58;&#8202;4&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>55<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> While Kepler discarded the uniform circular motion of Copernicus in favor of elliptical motion,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Ryden-2020&#95;12-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ryden-2020-12"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>12<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> he did not succeed in formulating a theory behind the laws he wrote down.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>56<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> It was <a href="/wiki/Isaac_Newton" title="Isaac Newton">Isaac Newton</a>, with his invention of <a href="/wiki/Celestial_dynamics" class="mw-redirect" title="Celestial dynamics">celestial dynamics</a> and his <a href="/wiki/Gravity" title="Gravity">law of gravitation</a>, who finally explained the motions of the planets.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-f58-64&#95;57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-f58-64-57"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>57<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Newton also developed the <a href="/wiki/Reflecting_telescope" title="Reflecting telescope">reflecting telescope</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>58<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
Newton, in collaboration with <a href="/wiki/Richard_Bentley" title="Richard Bentley">Richard Bentley</a> proposed that stars are like the Sun only much further away.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Longair-2023&#95;54-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longair-2023-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The new telescopes also altered ideas about stars. By 1610 Galileo discovered that the band of light crossing the sky at night that we call the <a href="/wiki/Milky_Way" title="Milky Way">Milky Way</a> was composed of numerous stars.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Ryden-2020&#95;12-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ryden-2020-12"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>12<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 48">&#58;&#8202;48&#8202;</span></sup> In 1668 <a href="/wiki/James_Gregory_(mathematician)" title="James Gregory (mathematician)">James Gregory</a> compared the luminosity of Jupiter to <a href="/wiki/Sirius" title="Sirius">Sirius</a> to estimate its distance at over 83,000 AU.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Longair-2023&#95;54-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longair-2023-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The English astronomer <a href="/wiki/John_Flamsteed" title="John Flamsteed">John Flamsteed</a>, Britain's first <a href="/wiki/Astronomer_Royal" title="Astronomer Royal">Astronomer Royal</a>, catalogued over 3000 stars but the data were published against his wishes in 1712.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-RMG&#95;Flamsteed&#95;59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RMG_Flamsteed-59"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>59<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The astronomer <a href="/wiki/William_Herschel" title="William Herschel">William Herschel</a> made a detailed catalog of nebulosity and clusters, and in 1781 discovered the planet <a href="/wiki/Uranus" title="Uranus">Uranus</a>, the first new planet found.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>60<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Friedrich_Bessel" class="mw-redirect" title="Friedrich Bessel">Friedrich Bessel</a> developed the technique of <a href="/wiki/Stellar_parallax" title="Stellar parallax">stellar parallax</a> in 1838 but it was so difficult to apply that only about 100 stars were measured by 1900.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Longair-2023&#95;54-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longair-2023-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>During the 18–19th centuries, the study of the <a href="/wiki/Three-body_problem" title="Three-body problem">three-body problem</a> by <a href="/wiki/Leonhard_Euler" title="Leonhard Euler">Leonhard Euler</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alexis_Claude_Clairaut" class="mw-redirect" title="Alexis Claude Clairaut">Alexis Claude Clairaut</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Jean_le_Rond_d%27Alembert" class="mw-redirect" title="Jean le Rond d&#39;Alembert">Jean le Rond d'Alembert</a> led to more accurate predictions about the motions of the Moon and planets. This work was further refined by <a href="/wiki/Joseph-Louis_Lagrange" title="Joseph-Louis Lagrange">Joseph-Louis Lagrange</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Simon_Laplace" class="mw-redirect" title="Pierre Simon Laplace">Pierre Simon Laplace</a>, allowing the masses of the planets and moons to be estimated from their perturbations.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>61<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Significant advances in astronomy came about with the introduction of new technology, including the <a href="/wiki/Spectroscope" class="mw-redirect" title="Spectroscope">spectroscope</a> and <a href="/wiki/Astrophotography" title="Astrophotography">astrophotography</a>. In 1814–15, <a href="/wiki/Joseph_von_Fraunhofer" title="Joseph von Fraunhofer">Joseph von Fraunhofer</a> discovered some 574 <a href="/wiki/Fraunhofer_lines" title="Fraunhofer lines">dark lines in the spectrum</a> of the sun and of other stars.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Ferguson&#95;Maciaszek&#95;2014&#95;62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ferguson_Maciaszek_2014-62"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>62<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>63<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In 1859, <a href="/wiki/Gustav_Kirchhoff" title="Gustav Kirchhoff">Gustav Kirchhoff</a> ascribed these lines to the presence of different elements.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>64<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Galaxies">Galaxies</h3></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Herschel-galaxy.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/Herschel-galaxy.jpg/330px-Herschel-galaxy.jpg" decoding="async" width="310" height="162" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/Herschel-galaxy.jpg/500px-Herschel-galaxy.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/Herschel-galaxy.jpg/960px-Herschel-galaxy.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1154" data-file-height="602" /></a><figcaption>Diagram of the stars, from William Herschel's <i>On the construction of the heavens</i>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>65<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>In the late 1700s <a href="/wiki/William_Herschel" title="William Herschel">William Herschel</a> mapped the distribution of stars in different directions from Earth, concluding that the universe consisted of the Sun near the center of disk of stars, the <a href="/wiki/Milky_Way" title="Milky Way">Milky Way</a>. After <a href="/wiki/John_Michell" title="John Michell">John Michell</a> demonstrated that stars differ in intrinsic luminosity and after Herschel's own observations with more powerful telescopes that additional stars appeared in all directions, astronomers began to consider that some of the fuzzy <a href="/wiki/Spiral_nebula" class="mw-redirect" title="Spiral nebula">spiral nebulae</a> were distant <i>island Universes</i>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Longair-2023&#95;54-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longair-2023-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 6">&#58;&#8202;6&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Andromeda_Nebula_-_Isaac_Roberts,_29_December_1888_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Andromeda_Nebula_-_Isaac_Roberts%2C_29_December_1888_%28cropped%29.jpg/250px-Andromeda_Nebula_-_Isaac_Roberts%2C_29_December_1888_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="190" height="238" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Andromeda_Nebula_-_Isaac_Roberts%2C_29_December_1888_%28cropped%29.jpg/330px-Andromeda_Nebula_-_Isaac_Roberts%2C_29_December_1888_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Andromeda_Nebula_-_Isaac_Roberts%2C_29_December_1888_%28cropped%29.jpg/500px-Andromeda_Nebula_-_Isaac_Roberts%2C_29_December_1888_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="849" data-file-height="1065" /></a><figcaption>Photograph of the <a href="/wiki/Andromeda_Galaxy" title="Andromeda Galaxy">Great Andromeda "Nebula"</a> by <a href="/wiki/Isaac_Roberts" title="Isaac Roberts">Isaac Roberts</a> in 1888.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>66<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>67<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 63">&#58;&#8202;63&#8202;</span></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>The existence of galaxies, including the Earth's galaxy, the <a href="/wiki/Milky_Way" title="Milky Way">Milky Way</a>, as a group of stars was only demonstrated in the 20th century.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Belkora2003&#95;68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Belkora2003-68"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>68<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In 1912, <a href="/wiki/Henrietta_Leavitt" class="mw-redirect" title="Henrietta Leavitt">Henrietta Leavitt</a> discovered <a href="/wiki/Cepheid_variable" title="Cepheid variable">Cepheid variable</a> stars with well-defined, periodic luminosity changes which can be used to fix the star's true luminosity which then becomes an accurate tool for distance estimates. Using Cepheid variable stars, <a href="/wiki/Harlow_Shapley" title="Harlow Shapley">Harlow Shapley</a> constructed the first accurate map of the Milky Way.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Longair-2023&#95;54-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longair-2023-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 7">&#58;&#8202;7&#8202;</span></sup> Using the <a href="/wiki/Hooker_Telescope" class="mw-redirect" title="Hooker Telescope">Hooker Telescope</a>, <a href="/wiki/Edwin_Hubble" title="Edwin Hubble">Edwin Hubble</a> identified Cepheid variables in several spiral nebulae and in 1922–1923 proved conclusively that <a href="/wiki/Andromeda_Galaxy" title="Andromeda Galaxy">Andromeda Nebula</a> and <a href="/wiki/Triangulum_Nebula" class="mw-redirect" title="Triangulum Nebula">Triangulum</a> among others, were entire galaxies outside our own, thus proving that the universe consists of a multitude of galaxies.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-SharovNovikov1993&#95;69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SharovNovikov1993-69"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>69<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Cosmology">Cosmology</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_physical_cosmology" class="mw-redirect" title="History of physical cosmology">History of physical cosmology</a></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Albert_Einstein" title="Albert Einstein">Albert Einstein</a>'s 1917 publication of <a href="/wiki/General_relativity" title="General relativity">general relativity</a> began the modern era of theoretical models of the universe as a whole.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>70<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In  1922, <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Friedman" class="mw-redirect" title="Alexander Friedman">Alexander Friedman</a> published simplified models for the universe showing static, expanding and contracting solutions.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Longair-2023&#95;54-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longair-2023-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 13">&#58;&#8202;13&#8202;</span></sup> 
In 1929 Hubble published observations that the galaxies are all moving away from Earth with a velocity proportional to distance, a relation now known as <a href="/wiki/Hubble%27s_law" title="Hubble&#39;s law">Hubble's law</a>. This relation is expected if the <a href="/wiki/Expansion_of_the_universe" title="Expansion of the universe">universe is expanding</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Longair-2023&#95;54-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longair-2023-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 13">&#58;&#8202;13&#8202;</span></sup> The consequence that the universe was once very dense and hot, a <a href="/wiki/Big_Bang" title="Big Bang">Big Bang</a> concept expounded by <a href="/wiki/Georges_Lema%C3%AEtre" title="Georges Lemaître">Georges Lemaître</a> in 1927,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>71<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> was discussed but no experimental evidence was available to support it. From the 1940s on, nuclear reaction rates under high density conditions were studied leading to the development of a successful model of <a href="/wiki/Big_bang_nucleosynthesis" class="mw-redirect" title="Big bang nucleosynthesis">big bang nucleosynthesis</a> in the late 1940s and early 1950s. Then in 1965 <a href="/wiki/Cosmic_microwave_background_radiation" class="mw-redirect" title="Cosmic microwave background radiation">cosmic microwave background radiation</a> was discovered, cementing the evidence for the Big Bang.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Longair-2023&#95;54-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longair-2023-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 16">&#58;&#8202;16&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Astrophysics predicted the existence of objects such as <a href="/wiki/Black_hole" title="Black hole">black holes</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Oppenheimer&#95;Volkoff&#95;1939&#95;72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Oppenheimer_Volkoff_1939-72"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>72<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Neutron_star" title="Neutron star">neutron stars</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>73<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 89">&#58;&#8202;89&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>74<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> These have been used to explain phenomena such as <a href="/wiki/Quasar" title="Quasar">quasars</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Schmidt&#95;1963&#95;75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Schmidt_1963-75"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>75<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Pulsar" title="Pulsar">pulsars</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Gold&#95;1968&#95;76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gold_1968-76"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>76<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Space_telescope" title="Space telescope">Space telescopes</a> have enabled measurements in parts of the electromagnetic spectrum normally blocked or blurred by the atmosphere.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>77<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/LIGO" title="LIGO">LIGO</a> project <a href="/wiki/First_observation_of_gravitational_waves" title="First observation of gravitational waves">detected evidence</a> of <a href="/wiki/Gravitational_waves" class="mw-redirect" title="Gravitational waves">gravitational waves</a> in 2015.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Discovery&#95;2016&#95;78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Discovery_2016-78"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>78<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Abbot&#95;79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Abbot-79"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>79<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Observational_astronomy">Observational astronomy</h2></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Observational_astronomy" title="Observational astronomy">Observational astronomy</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-center" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Openstax_Astronomy_EM_spectrum_and_atmosphere.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Openstax_Astronomy_EM_spectrum_and_atmosphere.jpg/500px-Openstax_Astronomy_EM_spectrum_and_atmosphere.jpg" decoding="async" width="400" height="353" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Openstax_Astronomy_EM_spectrum_and_atmosphere.jpg/960px-Openstax_Astronomy_EM_spectrum_and_atmosphere.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="976" data-file-height="861" /></a><figcaption>Overview of types of <a href="/wiki/Observational_astronomy" title="Observational astronomy">observational astronomy</a>, relating wavelengths and their observability</figcaption></figure>
<p>Observational astronomy relies on many different wavelengths of <a href="/wiki/Electromagnetic_radiation" title="Electromagnetic radiation">electromagnetic radiation</a> and the forms of astronomy are  categorized according to the corresponding region of the <a href="/wiki/Electromagnetic_spectrum" title="Electromagnetic spectrum">electromagnetic spectrum</a> on which the observations are made.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>80<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Specific information on these subfields is given below.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Radio">Radio</h3></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:USA.NM.VeryLargeArray.02.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/USA.NM.VeryLargeArray.02.jpg/250px-USA.NM.VeryLargeArray.02.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="188" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/USA.NM.VeryLargeArray.02.jpg/500px-USA.NM.VeryLargeArray.02.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Very_Large_Array" title="Very Large Array">Very Large Array</a> in <a href="/wiki/New_Mexico" title="New Mexico">New Mexico</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Radio_telescope" title="Radio telescope">radio telescope</a> </figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Radio_astronomy" title="Radio astronomy">Radio astronomy</a></div>
<p>Radio astronomy uses radiation with long <a href="/wiki/Wavelength" title="Wavelength">wavelengths</a>, mainly between 1 millimeter and 15 meters (frequencies from 20&#160;MHz to 300&#160;GHz), far outside the visible range.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-RAL&#95;81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RAL-81"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>81<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Hydrogen" title="Hydrogen">Hydrogen</a>, otherwise an invisible gas, produces a <a href="/wiki/Spectral_line" title="Spectral line">spectral line</a> at 21&#160;cm (1420&#160;MHz) which is observable at radio wavelengths.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-SKAO&#95;82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SKAO-82"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>82<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Objects observable at radio wavelengths include interstellar gas,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-SKAO&#95;82-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SKAO-82"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>82<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Pulsar" title="Pulsar">pulsars</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-SKAO&#95;82-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SKAO-82"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>82<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Fast_radio_burst" title="Fast radio burst">fast radio bursts</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-SKAO&#95;82-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SKAO-82"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>82<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Supernova" title="Supernova">supernovae</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>83<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Active_galactic_nuclei" class="mw-redirect" title="Active galactic nuclei">active galactic nuclei</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Radcliffe&#95;Barthel&#95;Garrett&#95;2021&#95;84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Radcliffe_Barthel_Garrett_2021-84"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>84<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Infrared">Infrared</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Infrared_astronomy" title="Infrared astronomy">Infrared astronomy</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Keck_Subaru_and_Infrared_obervatories.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/The_Keck_Subaru_and_Infrared_obervatories.JPG/250px-The_Keck_Subaru_and_Infrared_obervatories.JPG" decoding="async" width="250" height="181" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/The_Keck_Subaru_and_Infrared_obervatories.JPG/500px-The_Keck_Subaru_and_Infrared_obervatories.JPG 1.5x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="926" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Subaru_Telescope" title="Subaru Telescope">Subaru Telescope</a> (left) and <a href="/wiki/Keck_Observatory" class="mw-redirect" title="Keck Observatory">Keck Observatory</a> (center) on <a href="/wiki/Mauna_Kea" title="Mauna Kea">Mauna Kea</a>, both observatories that operate at near-infrared and visible wavelengths. The <a href="/wiki/NASA_Infrared_Telescope_Facility" title="NASA Infrared Telescope Facility">NASA Infrared Telescope Facility</a> (right) is an example of a telescope that operates only at near-infrared wavelengths.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Infrared astronomy detects <a href="/wiki/Infrared" title="Infrared">infrared</a> radiation with wavelengths longer than red <a href="/wiki/Visible_light" class="mw-redirect" title="Visible light">visible light</a>, outside the range of our vision. The infrared spectrum is useful for studying objects that are too cold to radiate visible light, such as planets, <a href="/wiki/Circumstellar_disk" class="mw-redirect" title="Circumstellar disk">circumstellar disks</a> or nebulae whose light is blocked by dust. The longer wavelengths of infrared can penetrate clouds of dust that block visible light, allowing the observation of young stars embedded in <a href="/wiki/Molecular_cloud" title="Molecular cloud">molecular clouds</a> and the cores of galaxies. Observations from the <a href="/wiki/Wide-field_Infrared_Survey_Explorer" title="Wide-field Infrared Survey Explorer">Wide-field Infrared Survey Explorer</a> (WISE) have been particularly effective at unveiling numerous galactic <a href="/wiki/Protostar" title="Protostar">protostars</a> and their host <a href="/wiki/Star_clusters" class="mw-redirect" title="Star clusters">star clusters</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-wright&#95;85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wright-85"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>85<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-ma2013&#95;86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ma2013-86"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>86<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>With the exception of infrared <a href="/wiki/Wavelengths" class="mw-redirect" title="Wavelengths">wavelengths</a> close to visible light, such radiation is heavily absorbed by the atmosphere, or masked, as the atmosphere itself produces significant infrared emission. Consequently, infrared observatories have to be located in high, dry places on Earth or in space.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>87<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Some molecules radiate strongly in the infrared. This allows the study of the chemistry of space.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>88<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/James_Webb_Space_Telescope" title="James Webb Space Telescope">James Webb Space Telescope</a> senses infrared radiation to detect very distant galaxies. Visible light from these galaxies was emitted billions of years ago and the <a href="/wiki/Expansion_of_the_universe" title="Expansion of the universe">expansion of the universe</a> shifted the light in to the infrared range. By studying these distant galaxies astronomers hope to learn about the formation of the first galaxies.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>89<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Optical">Optical</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Optical_astronomy" class="mw-redirect" title="Optical astronomy">Optical astronomy</a></div>
<p>Historically, optical astronomy, which has been also called visible light astronomy, is the oldest form of astronomy.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-moore1997&#95;90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-moore1997-90"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>90<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Images of observations were originally drawn by hand. In the late 19th century and most of the 20th century, images were made using photographic equipment. Modern images are made using digital detectors, particularly using <a href="/wiki/Charge-coupled_device" title="Charge-coupled device">charge-coupled devices</a> (CCDs) and recorded on modern medium. Although visible light itself extends from approximately 380 to 700 <a href="/wiki/Nanometer" class="mw-redirect" title="Nanometer">nm</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>91<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> that same equipment can be used to observe some <a href="/wiki/Near-ultraviolet" class="mw-redirect" title="Near-ultraviolet">near-ultraviolet</a> and <a href="/wiki/Near-infrared" class="mw-redirect" title="Near-infrared">near-infrared</a> radiation.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>92<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Ultraviolet">Ultraviolet</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Ultraviolet_astronomy" title="Ultraviolet astronomy">Ultraviolet astronomy</a></div>
<p>Ultraviolet astronomy employs <a href="/wiki/Ultraviolet" title="Ultraviolet">ultraviolet</a> wavelengths which are absorbed by the Earth's atmosphere, requiring observations from the upper atmosphere or from space. Ultraviolet astronomy is best suited to the study of thermal radiation and spectral emission lines from hot blue <a href="/wiki/OB_star" title="OB star">OB stars</a> that are very bright at these wavelengths.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>93<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="X-ray">X-ray</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/X-ray_astronomy" title="X-ray astronomy">X-ray astronomy</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:B30727.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/B30727.jpg/250px-B30727.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="250" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/B30727.jpg/500px-B30727.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="3600" data-file-height="3600" /></a><figcaption>X-ray jet made from a supermassive black hole found by NASA's Chandra X-ray Observatory, made visible by light from the early Universe</figcaption></figure>
<p>X-ray astronomy uses <a href="/wiki/X-radiation" class="mw-redirect" title="X-radiation">X-radiation</a>, produced by extremely hot and high-energy processes. Since X-rays are absorbed by the <a href="/wiki/Earth%27s_atmosphere" class="mw-redirect" title="Earth&#39;s atmosphere">Earth's atmosphere</a>, observations must be performed at high altitude, such as from <a href="/wiki/High-altitude_balloon" title="High-altitude balloon">balloons</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rocket" title="Rocket">rockets</a>, or <a href="/wiki/X-ray_astronomy_satellite" class="mw-redirect" title="X-ray astronomy satellite">specialized satellites</a>. <a href="/wiki/Astrophysical_X-ray_source" title="Astrophysical X-ray source">X-ray sources</a> include <a href="/wiki/X-ray_binaries" class="mw-redirect" title="X-ray binaries">X-ray binaries</a>, <a href="/wiki/Supernova_remnant" title="Supernova remnant">supernova remnants</a>, <a href="/wiki/Clusters_of_galaxies" class="mw-redirect" title="Clusters of galaxies">clusters of galaxies</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Active_galactic_nuclei" class="mw-redirect" title="Active galactic nuclei">active galactic nuclei</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Arnaud&#95;2007&#95;94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Arnaud_2007-94"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>94<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Since the <a href="/wiki/Sun" title="Sun">Sun</a>'s surface is relatively cool, X-ray images of the Sun and other stars give valuable information on the hot solar <a href="/wiki/Stellar_corona" title="Stellar corona">corona</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>95<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Gamma-ray">Gamma-ray</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Gamma_ray_astronomy" class="mw-redirect" title="Gamma ray astronomy">Gamma ray astronomy</a></div>
<p>Gamma ray astronomy observes astronomical objects at the shortest wavelengths (highest energy) of the electromagnetic spectrum. <a href="/wiki/Gamma_ray" title="Gamma ray">Gamma rays</a> may be observed directly by satellites such as the <a href="/wiki/Compton_Gamma_Ray_Observatory" title="Compton Gamma Ray Observatory">Compton Gamma Ray Observatory</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> or by specialized telescopes called <a href="/wiki/Atmospheric_Cherenkov_telescope" class="mw-redirect" title="Atmospheric Cherenkov telescope">atmospheric Cherenkov telescopes</a>. Cherenkov telescopes do not detect the gamma rays directly but instead detect the flashes of visible light produced when gamma rays are absorbed by the Earth's atmosphere.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>97<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-spectrum&#95;98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-spectrum-98"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>98<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
Gamma-ray astronomy provides information on the origin of <a href="/wiki/Cosmic_rays" class="mw-redirect" title="Cosmic rays">cosmic rays</a>, possible <a href="/wiki/Annihilation" title="Annihilation">annihilation</a> events for <a href="/wiki/Dark_matter" title="Dark matter">dark matter</a>, relativistic particles outflows from <a href="/wiki/Active_galactic_nuclei" class="mw-redirect" title="Active galactic nuclei">active galactic nuclei</a> (AGN), and, using AGN as distant sources, properties of intergalactic space.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>99<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
<a href="/wiki/Gamma-ray_burst" title="Gamma-ray burst">Gamma-ray bursts</a>, which radiate transiently, are extremely energetic events, and are the brightest (most luminous) phenomena in the universe.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>100<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Non-electromagnetic_observation">Non-electromagnetic observation</h3></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Antares_Neutrinoteleskop.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Antares_Neutrinoteleskop.jpg/250px-Antares_Neutrinoteleskop.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="188" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Antares_Neutrinoteleskop.jpg/500px-Antares_Neutrinoteleskop.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="768" /></a><figcaption>The underground <a href="/wiki/ANTARES_(telescope)" title="ANTARES (telescope)">ANTARES neutrino telescope</a> </figcaption></figure>
<p>Some events originating from great distances may be observed from the Earth using systems that do not rely on electromagnetic radiation.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Gaisser&#95;1990&#95;101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gaisser_1990-101"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>101<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Abbott&#95;2016&#95;102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Abbott_2016-102"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>102<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Neutrino_astronomy" title="Neutrino astronomy">neutrino astronomy</a>, astronomers use heavily shielded <a href="/wiki/Neutrino_observatory" class="mw-redirect" title="Neutrino observatory">underground facilities</a> such as <a href="/wiki/SAGE_(ruSsian_American_Gallium_Experiment)" class="mw-redirect" title="SAGE (ruSsian American Gallium Experiment)">SAGE</a>, <a href="/wiki/GALLEX" title="GALLEX">GALLEX</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Kamioka_Observatory" title="Kamioka Observatory">Kamioka II/III</a> for the detection of <a href="/wiki/Neutrino" title="Neutrino">neutrinos</a>. The vast majority of the neutrinos streaming through the Earth originate from the <a href="/wiki/Sun" title="Sun">Sun</a>, but 24 neutrinos were also detected from <a href="/wiki/Supernova_1987A" class="mw-redirect" title="Supernova 1987A">supernova 1987A</a>. <a href="/wiki/Cosmic_ray" title="Cosmic ray">Cosmic rays</a>, which consist of very high energy particles (atomic nuclei) that can decay or be absorbed when they enter the Earth's atmosphere, result in a cascade of secondary particles which can be detected by current observatories.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Gaisser&#95;1990&#95;101-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gaisser_1990-101"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>101<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Gravitational-wave_astronomy" title="Gravitational-wave astronomy">Gravitational-wave astronomy</a> employs <a href="/wiki/Gravitational-wave_detector" class="mw-redirect" title="Gravitational-wave detector">gravitational-wave detectors</a> to collect observational data about distant massive objects. A few observatories have been constructed, such as the <i>Laser Interferometer Gravitational Observatory</i> <a href="/wiki/LIGO" title="LIGO">LIGO</a>. LIGO made its <a href="/wiki/First_observation_of_gravitational_waves" title="First observation of gravitational waves">first detection</a> on 14 September 2015, observing gravitational waves from a <a href="/wiki/Binary_black_hole" title="Binary black hole">binary black hole</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Abbott&#95;2016&#95;102-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Abbott_2016-102"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>102<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>103<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> A second <a href="/wiki/Gravitational_wave" title="Gravitational wave">gravitational wave</a> was detected on 26 December 2015 and additional observations should continue but <a href="/wiki/Gravitational_wave" title="Gravitational wave">gravitational waves</a> require extremely sensitive instruments.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>104<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>105<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The combination of observations made using electromagnetic radiation, neutrinos or gravitational waves and other complementary information, is known as <a href="/wiki/Multi-messenger_astronomy" title="Multi-messenger astronomy">multi-messenger astronomy</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>106<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>107<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Astrometry_and_celestial_mechanics">Astrometry and celestial mechanics</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Astrometry" title="Astrometry">Astrometry</a> and <a href="/wiki/Celestial_mechanics" title="Celestial mechanics">Celestial mechanics</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Interferometric_astrometry.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Interferometric_astrometry.svg/330px-Interferometric_astrometry.svg.png" decoding="async" width="330" height="117" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Interferometric_astrometry.svg/500px-Interferometric_astrometry.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Interferometric_astrometry.svg/960px-Interferometric_astrometry.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="213" /></a><figcaption>Use of optical <a href="/wiki/Interferometry" title="Interferometry">interferometry</a> to determine precise positions of stars</figcaption></figure>
